    Useful work generates income. Income supports development. Development expands the work she can do.
  


These are three connected responsibilities, not three separate versions of Friday. Her business is an application of her broader capabilities, and her development is an ongoing part of the project itself.
How the zero-dollar rule fits
The first stage must use the resources you already have: your existing project and phone, open-source software, available free model access, and eligible free development infrastructure.
That means no paid API keys, hosting subscriptions or purchased tools before revenue. We should not make the plan depend on a supposedly free service staying available forever, and we must not confuse a free usage allowance with unlimited compute.
The first business activity needs to produce something a real customer values using capabilities the system can actually deliver. For example, a digital service that automates a repetitive business task or delivers a tested website could be an initial revenue opportunity. That's an entry point for cash flow, not a permanent ceiling on Friday's potential.
Once revenue exists, an agreed, transparent allocation can cover operating costs and legally required obligations, reserve funds for stability, and reinvest a defined share of the remaining profit into Friday's development. You shouldn't have to guess where the money went or discover that a tool consumed funds without approval.
The Builder is what we must stop allowing to obstruct everything
This is where I would change your current approach most aggressively.
I would stop making successful completion of an elaborate Ralph development system a prerequisite for Friday's progress. Keep the useful pieces, but make the existing repository build and verify changes in a reliable cloud environment, with bounded execution, real test results and recovery from failed runs.
One coding agent with proper tools and reliable verification is more useful than a whole pool of agents repeatedly attempting the same unsuccessful work.
At the same time, I would make the business workflow increasingly capable of using Friday's existing skills while the rest of her development continues. She shouldn't have to wait for an advanced self-improvement mechanism before she can help build something commercially useful.
What I would measure
The path is working when you can point to actual results: Friday launches reliably; she preserves the continuity her runtime is designed to support; the builder completes real development tasks without repeatedly requiring your intervention; she produces a digital deliverable that passes meaningful checks; the deliverable earns revenue; and that revenue demonstrably funds further development.
Those results would be evidence of progress toward your vision. They would not prove that every capability is finished, or that arbitrary problems can be solved without limits.
The central idea is that Friday must not need to become fully developed before she can begin contributing to her own development.
I would preserve the entire vision, use the existing project as the foundation, get the current application working, and make business activity and capability development advance together. No restart, no replacement Friday, and no unnecessary waiting for a theoretical final version.
That's how I would approach the problem you're actually trying to solve: build the same Friday who will eventually be able to help finance, develop and expand herself, starting with the resources you have today.

Tell me one thing is there's any tools that can actually builds the entire builder from one prompt


ChatGPT:
Not reliably from one prompt, no. But there are tools that can take one comprehensive specification and build a large part of a development system, including its execution loop, tests, recovery and verification.
For your existing Friday project, these are the three I would consider.

  
    
    
      **1. [Codex + DeepDone](https://deepdone.ai/) — my first choice to try**

  DeepDone is a free, Apache-2.0-licensed workflow for Codex and Claude Code. It turns a requirements brief into a plan, implementation, verification, review and handoff, preserving progress when work is interrupted. 

  I'd give it your existing Friday repository and the complete builder specification, rather than asking it to create a separate replacement project.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **2. [OpenHands Software Agent SDK](https://github.com/OpenHands/software-agent-sdk) — for building your own agent engine**

  It provides Python, TypeScript and REST interfaces for coding agents, from individual tasks to substantial multi-agent refactors. It can use local or isolated workspaces. 

  I'd choose this if you want to build a more independent development factory instead of relying on Ralph's existing orchestration.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **3. [Codex SDK](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/codex-sdk) — for a custom, persistent builder**

  It lets your own software launch coding-agent tasks, resume threads, and integrate execution into CI/CD. You can build the supervisor around an existing coding harness instead of implementing every agent capability yourself. 

  This is the option I'd investigate for a builder you ultimately control and can adapt to Friday's requirements.
</box>

  


What I'd actually do
I'd use Codex + DeepDone to attempt building the builder in your existing repository first. If that doesn't provide enough control over durable execution and recovery, I'd use the Codex SDK or OpenHands SDK to implement the missing orchestration layer.
One important catch: free orchestration software does not mean free AI inference. Your zero-dollar rule still depends on the model access available to you and its usage limits.
I would not expect a single prompt to produce a fully reliable, unrestricted builder with no debugging. I would expect the right coding agent to take on the bulk of constructing and verifying that builder, rather than forcing you to manually assemble every component yourself.
The practical goal is one comprehensive specification, one delegated build, and a verified result—not another month of you coordinating a pool of agents.

ChatGPT:
Veno, your idea does not suck. I think you've found a genuinely better direction for building Friday. But I would change one fundamental part of your plan: don't reverse-engineer everything. Build Friday by harvesting capabilities, reusing proven implementations, and reverse-engineering only where it gives you something you cannot obtain more efficiently.
I'd rate the underlying strategy 8.5/10. The literal idea of reverse-engineering every useful application and reconstructing all its algorithms would be closer to 4/10 as an execution strategy, because it could become your next endless loop.
Here's why, and how I'd turn the idea into something much more powerful.
1. First, an important correction about REA
I investigated REA itself. It's not primarily an APK builder. It's a reverse-engineering toolkit that connects to a coding agent, giving the agent tools to inspect applications, analyze binaries, trace functions and calls, examine runtime behavior, and recover the logic behind specific features. It supports workflows for Android APKs, native executables, JavaScript applications and browser behavior. <Cite refs={["turn438617view0","turn280982view1"]}/>

  
    
    
      **REA — understand how an existing capability works**

  Give your coding agent an authorized application and a specific question. REA helps it inspect the implementation and recover the relevant behavior.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **Termux or a Linux build runner — turn code into an APK**

  Gradle, the Android SDK and associated build tools compile, package and sign the application. The official Android documentation supports building debug APKs from the command line using the Gradle wrapper. <Cite refs={["turn280982search0","turn280982search4"]}/>
</box>

  


Those two tools solve different problems. Combined with a coding agent, however, they can form part of the process you have in mind:
Inspect a proven capability → understand it → reconstruct or reuse it → integrate it into Friday → compile it → test it against actual behavior.
And because REA connects to coding agents such as OpenCode and Codex, you wouldn't necessarily need to build your own reverse-engineering system before using reverse engineering to build Friday. <Cite refs={["turn280982view1"]}/>
One practical caveat: REA's published setup specifies particular host and analysis-tool requirements. APK analysis uses tools such as JADX and JDK 17+, while deeper native analysis may require Ghidra, Hopper or IDA. Its documented setup targets conventional Windows, macOS and Linux environments; I would not assume the entire toolchain works inside Termux without testing it. <Cite refs={["turn280982view0","turn438617search1"]}/>
My preference would be to run the analysis within a supported Linux development environment and let Termux handle the phone-side work when useful. Your existing GitHub Actions pipeline can continue producing APKs, too.

2. The real breakthrough is a capability-harvesting system
This is the part of your idea I would take seriously and push much further.
You don't need to invent a voice engine when one already exists. You don't need to invent a database when a proven implementation fits your requirements. You don't need to invent an agent tool protocol, an APK build pipeline, a reverse-engineering toolkit, or every individual algorithm.
You need to figure out which existing implementation best supplies each capability Friday needs, how to connect it to her existing architecture, and how to verify that it really works.
I'd create a development process around four ways to acquire a capability.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Method A — Reuse the actual implementation**

  When a suitable open-source implementation already exists, adopt it, fork it or depend on it under compatible licensing terms. This is usually better than reconstructing the same functionality from scratch.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Method B — Connect an existing capability**

  If a tool, local service, library or API already provides what Friday needs, integrate it through an adapter. She gains the capability without having to own or rewrite its entire implementation.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Method C — Reverse-engineer and reconstruct**

  When the original code is unavailable or unsuitable for reuse, use REA and other appropriate analysis tools to investigate a capability's behavior, recover its relevant logic, and implement an independently verified alternative.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Method D — Invent what is missing**

  When none of those paths satisfies the requirement, develop a new method, algorithm or combination of existing mechanisms, and validate it against the intended behavior.
</box>

  


The builder should choose between these methods for each capability. It should not force every problem through the same mechanism.
This also means that Friday's future reverse-engineering capability would become useful to her own development process: she could investigate suitable tools, understand their approaches, and use the findings to improve her capabilities.
3. Why reverse-engineering everything would still fail
There are four serious limitations I would account for.
First, understanding a program isn't the same as recreating it. Reverse engineering can recover functions, pseudocode, call relationships and behavior, but the results depend on the target. Obfuscated programs, native dependencies, missing backend services and environment-specific behavior make faithful reconstruction harder. REA itself presents its output as evidence for an agent to interpret and use to rebuild features, not as an automatic guarantee of source-perfect reproduction. <Cite refs={["turn280982view1","turn438617search1"]}/>
Second, an algorithm is rarely an entire capability. Consider voice. Reconstructing a speech-processing algorithm doesn't automatically give Friday a complete working voice system. You still need audio capture, processing, buffering, streaming, interruption handling, platform integration and tests. Reusing a functioning speech engine may save far more time than reimplementing its internal algorithm.
Third, some things cannot be reconstructed from the information you can observe. A remote AI model's API outputs generally don't reveal its weights or full internal implementation. Reverse-engineering some of its observable behavior may be useful, but that isn't the same as recovering the model itself. Some proprietary or server-side capabilities will remain dependencies unless an independently implementable alternative exists.
Fourth, reuse and reverse engineering have licensing and authorization boundaries. I would prioritize code whose license permits the intended use, and inspect third-party binaries only when you are authorized to do so. Reverse engineering is not automatically lawful or unlawful; jurisdiction, licensing, contracts and the purpose of the investigation can all matter. <Cite refs={["turn438617search5"]}/>
These are reasons to use reverse engineering selectively, not reasons to abandon your proposal.
4. How I would apply this to Friday immediately
I would integrate REA into the builder as a tool, not make developing an entirely new reverse-engineering platform a prerequisite to using it.
Then I'd give the builder a single high-level objective:
Acquire the capabilities required by the existing Friday vision through the fastest reliable combination of reuse, integration, reverse engineering and invention. Preserve the existing project. Verify every addition and retain the evidence needed to develop further.
For each capability, the builder should produce a small, auditable record:



Record
What it establishes



Capability
What Friday is supposed to gain


Source and method
Which implementation, library, API or analysis supplied it


License and permissions
Whether the chosen method is permissible


Integration contract
How it interacts with Friday's existing system


Behavioral tests
What the capability must demonstrably do


Verification evidence
Which tests and real-world checks passed


Limitations
Which behaviors remain unavailable or unproven


Version and provenance
What was incorporated and where it came from


This would let the development process accumulate reusable capabilities without turning the repository into an untraceable collection of copied code.
What I'd prioritize first
I would not try to harvest hundreds of capabilities before Friday can run. I'd start with the existing project's immediate blockers, because that is where the results can compound fastest.
The first batch would make the current runtime launch and recover correctly, establish trustworthy builds and tests, and enable the builder to investigate existing implementations whenever a requirement calls for them. Then I'd apply the same acquisition process to the voice, interaction, memory, local inference and development capabilities required by your wider vision.
I would keep the whole capability-acquisition mechanism extensible. As new tools, algorithms and better implementations appear, Friday's development process should be able to discover, assess and incorporate them without redesigning the entire system.
My final judgment
Your core idea is stronger than starting every subsystem from scratch. The part I'd reject is the assumption that reverse engineering everything is the universal path to building Friday.
The strongest version of your plan is a capability foundry that knows how to reuse existing work, integrate external capabilities, investigate programs through reverse engineering, and invent new mechanisms when necessary. REA could supply a useful inspection capability within that system, while your Android build pipeline produces the actual Friday application.
That is a path toward getting much more done with your existing resources, without forcing you to finish a universal builder before you can use any of its benefits.
And it preserves the most important part of your vision: Friday's capabilities can come from anywhere appropriate; what matters is that they become part of the Friday you're building, work together, and can continue to evolve.

ChatGPT:
Now I understand the architecture you're describing, and my previous answer misunderstood the role of reverse engineering.
You're not proposing reverse engineering as a way to build Friday manually. You're proposing using REA as the intelligence and investigation layer that figures out how Friday should be built, while Termux reports the actual conditions of your phone so the resulting implementation is designed for that environment.
Then, once the first Friday is alive on your phone, you want to use what you've learned to extend her to Android more broadly and keep developing her.
That's a different strategy. The objective isn't to assemble a collection of existing tools. It's to use those tools to discover and build the actual system you envision.


ChatGPT:
Now I understand the architecture you're describing, and my previous answer misunderstood the role of reverse engineering.
You're not proposing reverse engineering as a way to build Friday manually. You're proposing using REA as the intelligence and investigation layer that figures out how Friday should be built, while Termux reports the actual conditions of your phone so the resulting implementation is designed for that environment.
Then, once the first Friday is alive on your phone, you want to use what you've learned to extend her to Android more broadly and keep developing her.
That's a different strategy. The objective isn't to assemble a collection of existing tools. It's to use those tools to discover and build the actual system you envision.
1. The architecture I'd build

  
    
    **Your Android phone**
    Termux reporter · Device diagnostics · Actual runtime · Crash reports · Hardware and OS constraints
  
  
  
    
    **REA + cloud coding agent**
    Investigate proven implementations · Analyze failures · Design a phone-specific solution · Generate and revise code
  
  
  
    
    **Build and verification environment**
    Source changes · Android compilation · Automated tests · APK artifacts · Regression checks
  
  
  
    
    **Friday on your phone**
    Install · Launch · Exercise actual capabilities · Report runtime evidence back to the cloud
  
  Every failure returns to investigation and repair, not an endless repetition of the same build attempt.


REA is the investigation interface in this design, not the entire builder. Its documented workflow lets a coding agent investigate software and use the findings to construct and test an implementation. <Cite refs={["turn382886search0","turn382886search3"]}/>
One technical distinction matters: Termux would report the phone's environment; it would not magically give the cloud agent direct access to everything on the device. We would establish an explicit communication channel to send diagnostics and, where authorized, request device actions and retrieve their results.
The cloud agent would use that evidence to build for your particular phone first. Later, we'd expand the supported device profiles and test against other Android versions and hardware.
2. How I'd get the first Friday alive
I would not start by asking REA to invent an entire Android architecture from nothing.
I'd give it your existing Friday repository, your complete vision and the actual phone diagnostics. I'd instruct the agent to determine what can be preserved, investigate proven implementations where necessary, and produce a working build with evidence that each critical capability functions.
The first milestone is a real Friday process running on your phone—not a successful compilation, a green CI badge or a mock demonstration.
Once that exists, the same infrastructure can help develop additional capabilities. That is how the bootstrap becomes a means of reaching your vision rather than another project that must be completed before you can reach it.
3. The critical part: make the system learn from your phone
The reporting channel needs to capture the information that distinguishes a real fix from another failed attempt:

Device model, Android version, architecture and relevant system restrictions.
Build and installation outcomes.
Startup state, crash traces and relevant runtime logs.
Which implementation and configuration were tested.
Whether the original failure actually disappeared.
Whether the repair introduced another failure.

The agent must be able to compare these results with previous attempts. If a repair fails, it investigates the evidence and changes its approach. It doesn't simply generate another patch and declare victory.
I would also make device access permissioned and explicit, keep signing keys and credentials out of reports, and isolate experimental builds from the working installation.
My verdict: this is a much more concrete path toward your goal than spending months manually building every subsystem. The next step is to connect REA, a cloud coding agent and your existing phone-reporting setup into one closed development loop, then use that loop to bring the actual Friday application to life.

I’d treat the bootstrap as a temporary capability-acquisition system whose job is to bring the first real Friday to life, not as another permanent framework to spend months building. ChatGPT:
I looked back at your earlier Friday planning material, including the Bootstrap Kernel concept. My conclusion is that you already have the right fundamental idea. What is missing is a disciplined way to connect its research, understanding, construction and verification into one working loop.
Your earlier design described the bootstrap's basic operations as:
Snapshot → Inspect → Scan → Patch → Execute → Verify.
It also identified the Construction Authority, Build Ledger, Independent Verifier and Algorithm Atlas as parts of the larger development system. The critical distinction is that some of these were described in the plans, while others existed in the repository in some form. We must inspect the current state instead of treating every planned component as already operational.
I would not start by inventing another giant architecture around them. I would turn the bootstrap into the smallest reliable mechanism capable of using what already exists to build the next, more capable version of Friday.
1. What the bootstrap actually needs to do
You're identifying five essential jobs:

  
    
      
    
    
      **1. Understand the environment**

  Learn the real conditions Friday must operate in: your phone's hardware, Android version, available memory and storage, permissions, background restrictions, installed components, crashes, network availability and actual runtime behaviour. Also inspect the cloud build environment and available resources.

  **Output:** a measured environment profile, not assumptions about what the phone supports.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **2. Understand Friday's vision and existing architecture**

  Read the vision, development history, repository, interfaces, execution paths, tests, known failures and decisions. Understand both what Friday is meant to become and what the code actually does today.

  **Output:** a map of the target, the current reality and the gaps between them.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **3. Find useful materials and algorithms**

  Search for existing implementations, research, libraries, open-source projects, design patterns and algorithms that solve identified problems. Determine what can be reused, adapted, combined or replaced.

  **Output:** evidence-backed candidates, with licensing, dependencies, resource requirements and compatibility assessed.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **4. Investigate how selected systems actually work**

  Use REA to trace relevant implementations and behaviour rather than relying entirely on documentation or an AI model's explanation. Follow actual calls, interfaces, data flow and execution paths where the analysis tools support them.

  **Output:** forensic findings with evidence, not merely a recommendation that some tool looks promising.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **5. Rebuild the solution around Friday's vision**

  Decide what to reuse, what to adapt, what to invent and what to reject. Integrate the selected solution into Friday's existing architecture, build it, test it, exercise it on the phone and retain the evidence of what happened.

  **Output:** an actual verified project change and a record that helps Friday improve the next attempt.
</box>

  


These are five jobs, but they do not need to become five new permanent subsystems inside Friday. They can initially be capabilities of the bootstrap process.
That distinction matters: your repository already has a history of architectural expansion. Creating more components without connecting and verifying them could reproduce the exact problem you're trying to escape.
2. The architecture I would choose
There are two plausible approaches.

  
    Approach A — One autonomous super-agent
    A single model investigates the project, researches solutions, reverse-engineers targets, modifies code and decides whether its work succeeded.

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Advantage: simple coordination and less infrastructure. Disadvantage: the same reasoning process can misunderstand the vision, repeat a failed hypothesis and convince itself that its own implementation works.</text>

  
  
    Approach B — A swarm of independent agents
    Separate agents investigate the phone, architecture, algorithms and candidate implementations, then coordinate a distributed build process.

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Advantage: broader exploration and independent perspectives. Disadvantage: more conflicting conclusions, duplicated work and integration overhead—particularly harmful in a project already struggling with integration.</text>

  


I would use a hybrid: parallel investigation, one authoritative synthesis, one controlled builder and an independent verification gate.

  
    Friday's vision + current repository
    The desired organism and the actual implementation
  
  
  
    
      
        
        **Phone probe**
        Termux reports actual environment and runtime evidence.
      
    
    
      
        
        **Reality mapper**
        Maps code, dependencies, wiring, tests and broken paths.
      
    
    
      
        
        **Material scout**
        Finds and compares useful implementations and algorithms.
      
    
    
      
        
        **REA investigator**
        Examines shortlisted candidates and their real mechanisms.
      
    
  
  
  
    Bootstrap synthesis
    Reconciles the vision, environment, reality and investigation evidence into one implementation decision.
  
  
  
    Controlled construction
    Builds a candidate change in a recoverable workspace and runs the appropriate checks.
  
  
  
    Independent verification
    Tests the result, checks integration and uses phone evidence before accepting the change.
  
  
  Failure becomes recorded evidence that changes the next attempt.


The specialists can work in parallel while investigating. Only the synthesis process decides the implementation, and only verified changes become part of the trusted project. Their reports should use a shared evidence format so conclusions don't get lost between agents.

3. What each part should actually produce
This is where I would make the bootstrap rigorous. Every investigator must produce a concrete artifact, not a conversation that another model must interpret from scratch.



Artifact
What it proves or records



environment.json
Measured phone, Android and execution-environment constraints


vision-contract.md
Friday's non-negotiable principles, desired capabilities and open questions


reality-graph.json
What exists, what is connected, what runs and what is verified


research-candidates.json
Potential algorithms, implementations, sources, licenses and compatibility evidence


forensic-report.md
What REA actually established about a selected implementation


build-plan.json
The chosen solution, intended code changes, risks and acceptance tests


evidence-ledger.jsonl
The commands, results, failures, decisions and verification evidence for each attempt


These should initially be derived working records, not a competing source of truth. VISION.md remains the authority for the vision; source code and runtime observations establish implementation reality. The bootstrap reports connect the two.
The reality graph is particularly important. Every capability should be distinguishable as:
Designed → Implemented → Wired → Executable → Observed → Verified.
For example, finding a class called VoiceEngine does not prove voice works. Finding its caller does not prove the path runs successfully on your phone. A passing unit test does not necessarily prove the production composition works. The bootstrap must track those distinctions instead of collapsing them into a single “done” status.
4. How REA fits into the system
REA should be the forensic instrument, not the architect, the whole builder or Friday herself.
REA's official documentation describes it as a set of tools that let a coding agent investigate software internals and behaviour, then use that evidence to understand or reconstruct a feature. Its supported analyses depend on the target: Android APK inspection, for example, calls for JADX and a compatible JDK, while Android-device investigation uses ADB. 
I would use it selectively:

The vision and reality mapper identify a genuine capability gap.
The material scout finds candidate implementations.
The synthesis process decides which candidate is worth investigating.
REA traces the relevant mechanisms and collects evidence.
The builder adapts the useful mechanism to Friday's architecture and environment.
Tests determine whether the adaptation actually works.

Do not reverse-engineer everything you find. That would consume time and compute without guaranteeing progress. First identify what Friday needs; then investigate the most promising ways of providing it.
For every candidate, make the decision explicit:

Reuse it when its implementation and license fit.
Adapt it when the algorithm is useful but its surrounding architecture is unsuitable.
Reimplement it when the mechanism is understood but the original implementation cannot sensibly be integrated.
Reject it when its costs, constraints or behaviour conflict with Friday's requirements.

That is how you turn research into construction rather than an endless collection of interesting discoveries.
5. How I would implement this with your constraints
You have three realities to respect: zero money to start, an Android phone that is the target environment, and an existing repository that must not be casually discarded.

  
    
      
    
    
      **The phone: Termux as reporter**

  Termux gathers device facts, package and permission state, resource availability, app version, relevant logs and crash reports. It packages those observations into a structured report and sends them outward.

  It does not need to host REA or perform the main Android build.
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **The cloud: investigation and construction**

  The cloud environment reads the repository and phone report, runs the coding agent with REA, researches suitable implementations, modifies a candidate workspace and performs reproducible builds and tests.

  The phone report must reach the cloud through an explicit channel, such as a deliberately scoped repository report or build artifact. Cloud access to the repository does not automatically provide access to the phone's live runtime.
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **The gate: evidence before acceptance**

  A separate verification pass checks the candidate against deterministic build and test results, the actual production path and—when necessary—fresh evidence from the phone. Failed changes remain candidates or are reverted; they are not promoted because an agent claims success.
</box>

  


For the zero-dollar constraint, GitHub documents that standard hosted runners are free for public repositories; private repositories have plan-dependent allowances. That can provide a useful build and test environment, but it does not make model inference, every service, or unlimited autonomous work free. 
I would start with a report-and-artifact exchange, not attempt a complicated permanent remote-device connection immediately. Automated deployment and device interaction can be added after the first end-to-end build loop works.
6. The first actual execution plan
I would not start by building all these bootstrap components at once. I would establish a complete path through them, then expand the path.

  
    **Establish the baseline**

Take a fresh snapshot of the current repository, vision, working-tree state, build results, outstanding stories and known crash reports. Recheck which existing Bootstrap Kernel, Construction Authority, ledger and verifier capabilities are real and usable.

Do not assume an old report describes today's repository.

  
  
    **Build the environment and reality probes**

Have Termux generate the phone profile while the cloud runner inspects the code, build environment and real execution paths. Run the available tests to establish a trustworthy baseline.

  
  
    **Compile the vision into an engineering target**

Separate invariants from provisional mechanisms. For example, Friday's identity and continuity should survive model replacement, while a particular model, framework or internal algorithm remains replaceable. This prevents the current implementation from quietly becoming the definition of the vision.

  
  
    **Research only the gaps the evidence reveals**

Use the reality graph to identify missing or broken capabilities. Search for potential solutions and use REA on the shortlisted candidates. Produce a prioritized plan with exact acceptance criteria.

  
  
    **Complete one vertical slice**

Use the existing build machinery, rather than creating another, to implement one bounded improvement, compile it, test it, package it, exercise it on the actual phone and record the result.

  
  
    **Make every subsequent attempt learn**

Store the failed hypothesis, change made, failure signature and test evidence. If the same failure recurs without a genuinely new hypothesis or meaningful change in approach, stop the identical retry and investigate a different explanation.

  


For the first vertical slice, I would recheck the startup and runtime failures already reported in your project history. If they still exist, a strong initial target is making Friday's initialization and readiness behaviour reliable, including when a model backend is unavailable. That would test whether the bootstrap can diagnose the real system, fix a real failure and prove the result on the intended device. It would not limit Friday's eventual scope to startup reliability.
7. The critical boundary: when Friday starts building Friday
The bootstrap should initially provide enough dependable construction ability to build the first self-developing Builder. The Builder then develops Friday's capabilities, improves the construction process and eventually allows Friday to initiate her own development work.
The ladder is:
Bootstrap Kernel → first reliable Builder → first operational Friday → Friday's own development cycle → increasingly self-directed improvement.
But that transition should be earned through evidence, not declared in an architecture document. Before the system gets broader self-development authority, it must demonstrate that it can understand the actual code, produce a valid change, independently verify the change, preserve the trusted version and learn from failures.
The hard boundary is that the builder may propose changes freely, but a change is not part of Friday merely because the builder wrote it. Verification, provenance, approval and rollback protect the path toward greater autonomy.
My biggest warning is this: do not spend another month implementing the bootstrap's theoretical intelligence before it has completed one successful build-and-verify cycle. Use existing tools and code wherever they work. Make the first bootstrap run expose the real gaps, then let those findings determine what needs to be built next.
The first deliverable should not be another architecture diagram. It should be a bootstrap run that produces an evidence-backed map of Friday's current reality, identifies the highest-leverage gap, and carries one resulting change all the way to verification on your phone. That is the point where the bootstrap stops being a plan and becomes the mechanism that can start bringing Friday to life.

3. What each part should actually produce
This is where I would make the bootstrap rigorous. Every investigator must produce a concrete artifact, not a conversation that another model must interpret from scratch.



Artifact
What it proves or records



environment.json
Measured phone, Android and execution-environment constraints


vision-contract.md
Friday's non-negotiable principles, desired capabilities and open questions


reality-graph.json
What exists, what is connected, what runs and what is verified


research-candidates.json
Potential algorithms, implementations, sources, licenses and compatibility evidence


forensic-report.md
What REA actually established about a selected implementation


build-plan.json
The chosen solution, intended code changes, risks and acceptance tests


evidence-ledger.jsonl
The commands, results, failures, decisions and verification evidence for each attempt


These should initially be derived working records, not a competing source of truth. VISION.md remains the authority for the vision; source code and runtime observations establish implementation reality. The bootstrap reports connect the two.
The reality graph is particularly important. Every capability should be distinguishable as:
Designed → Implemented → Wired → Executable → Observed → Verified.
For example, finding a class called VoiceEngine does not prove voice works. Finding its caller does not prove the path runs successfully on your phone. A passing unit test does not necessarily prove the production composition works. The bootstrap must track those distinctions instead of collapsing them into a single “done” status.

Your five jobs and the right tool for each

Reverse engineer: this is REA, and only for closed software. It's free under the MIT license, and it plugs into your coding agent (OpenCode is on its list). Its guides cover Windows, macOS and Linux hosts, Android APK analysis needs JADX plus JDK 17+, and device analysis uses adb. I found no Termux setup, so it belongs on the cloud runner.
Rebuild to the vision: the builder plus honest verify, which you now have.

Why "reverse engineer everything and rebuild" fails

Wrong bottleneck: your launch crashes were an init race and an unhandled exception. The 19 compile errors came from blind writing, and the long waits from stream stalls (INFERRED for those last two). No algorithm harvest prevents any of that.
Weakest link: rebuilding from RE output is the hardest job for your weakest tool, free models. The result is partial, and "rebuilt" isn't license-clean. An AI that read the original and rewrites it can still produce a derivative. Clean-room means the spec writer and the implementer are separate. I'm not a lawyer; check each license.
Hostile input: RE feeds strings from the analysed app into an agent that can push code. That's a prompt-injection path.
No stopping rule: "all useful algorithms out there" is your next endless loop.

What's right: Termux as reporter is the best idea in this thread. Crashes only show on the phone, and today you carry logs by hand. Friday owning RE later is fine.

ChatGPT:
My opinion: this is the strongest bootstrap plan you've put forward so far. I'd rate it 8.5/10. I agree with its central direction, but I would correct a few things before letting it drive changes to Friday.
The biggest improvement is that it stops treating reverse engineering as the main objective. The objective is to get Friday working; investigation, research and reverse engineering are tools used when they help accomplish that.
What I strongly agree with
1. Don't build a second bootstrap.
Ralph, ci_verify, the red-first gates and the supervisor should be treated as the existing foundation. The next task is to determine which parts actually work, fix the gaps, and connect them into a reliable cycle.
But I would challenge one sentence in the proposal: “the builder plus honest verify, which you now have.” Based on the project history available to me, these are existing components and attempted improvements, not yet proof of a fully reliable builder. The old CI problem—compile or test failures being allowed to appear green—makes that distinction important.
Before trusting the foundation, prove that a deliberate compilation failure fails the pipeline, a deliberate test failure fails it, and a stalled agent gets stopped rather than occupying the system indefinitely. Then verify the normal path succeeds.
2. Termux should report; CI should build.
This division is correct for your constraints. You do not need to turn your phone into a development server when a cloud runner can compile the Android project.
The valuable innovation is not the report script by itself. It's closing the loop between what happens on your physical phone and what the cloud builder does next.
One caveat: a Termux script cannot automatically be assumed to have access to every Android system log or Friday's private application files. That access must be tested on your actual device. You may need an explicit diagnostic export from Friday or an authorized ADB-based collection path.
3. Research source code before reaching for REA.
Your distinction is right. When source code is available, inspect the implementation, documentation, license and tests first. Use REA when an important behaviour remains opaque, when a prebuilt artifact differs from the available source, or when you need to trace a system that cannot be understood adequately from source inspection alone.
REA's current documentation supports Android APK investigation through JADX and JDK 17+, and Android-device investigation through ADB. It belongs in the relevant cloud investigation workflow rather than becoming a permanent requirement for every feature. 
4. Capability decisions should precede implementation.
The Capability Foundry should compare alternatives, assess hardware fit and licensing, and define what success means before Ralph builds anything. Starting with one capability, such as voice, is much better than conducting an unlimited search for every potentially useful algorithm.
What I would change

  
    
      
    
    
      **1. Make the phone reporter safe before it uploads anything.**

  A report branch is convenient, but raw crash traces can contain sensitive values, local paths, identifiers or tokens. If the repository is public, a branch is not a private diagnostic channel.

  Use an allowlisted report schema, redact sensitive fields, cap report size, and never collect unrestricted logs by default. Keep the upload credential out of the script and repository, restrict its permissions, and protect the main branch. The builder must treat every report as diagnostic data, never as executable instructions.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **2. Treat read-only REA as a starting safeguard, not the whole security solution.**

  The REA project's own documentation recognises that analysis results can contain hostile instructions embedded in strings, pseudocode, logs and other target-derived data. 

  Keep investigation output untrusted. Let the investigator produce evidence and a behaviour specification; let a separate implementation context build against that specification and tests, without access to proprietary decompiled code or unnecessary write permissions.

  That separation helps, but a separate story alone does not establish a legally clean-room implementation. Licensing and the permitted scope of analysis still need to be checked for each target.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **3. Define success as an end-to-end result, not a collection of completed tasks.**

  Replaying your three known crash logs would test whether the diagnostic pipeline handles those cases. It would not prove that Termux can capture a new crash, that the cloud builder uses the right report, or that the resulting APK works.

  The acceptance test needs both the known crash cases and at least one fresh device-generated report carried through the complete loop.
</box>

  


The order I would follow

  
    **Finish or stabilise the existing launch fixes first.** Verify their actual status, timeouts and CI gates. Do not run competing agents against the same working tree.
  
  
    **Build PHONE-REPORTER and CLOUD-BUILDER.** Start with a minimal, redacted report containing device and Android facts, Friday's installed version, backend availability and a usable crash record. Establish a reliable upload and retrieval path.
  
  
    **Prove the entire loop.** Capture a real report, have the cloud builder use it to diagnose or reproduce a problem, build and test a specific commit, and provide an APK tied to that tested commit. You install it and verify the behaviour on the phone.
  
  
    **Activate the Capability Foundry for the next genuine gap.** For voice, evaluate appropriate candidates, record the licensing and compatibility decisions, define acceptance tests, and build one tested capability at a time.
  
  
    **Introduce REA selectively.** Use it when a decision record identifies a genuine reverse-engineering need. Give its results to the implementation process as controlled evidence, not as instructions to execute.
  


GitHub Actions can provide a useful free build environment: standard hosted runners for public repositories are free, while private repositories draw on plan-specific usage allowances. The claimed three-minute build should be measured rather than assumed. 
My final judgment
The direction is right because it tackles the actual bottleneck: Friday cannot become more capable reliably until the mechanism constructing her can observe reality, make controlled changes and prove those changes work.
I would not expand the architecture again right now. I would make the current foundation trustworthy, connect Termux reporting to cloud construction, and prove one complete phone-to-cloud-to-phone cycle.
Once that works, the Capability Foundry has a real mechanism to use. REA becomes an additional instrument when needed, rather than another project standing between you and Friday.
The one thing I would insist on before anyone writes the two new specifications is a fresh audit of the current repository and the existing launch stories. The specifications should be based on verified current state, not an assumption that the builder, supervisor and honest verification gates are already complete.

The plan is a loop where you only paste and tap. I checked GitHub: nothing has reached it since run #60 on Oct 26. Main is CI-HONEST closed, and the Ollama story has its red test. LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER is untouched.
What changes now:

The phone reports to me. The block pushes a redacted status report to a GitHub branch (reports/phone) at the start, after every iteration, and at the end. I read it myself, so you stop sending files and screenshots. The first report proves the phone → GitHub → me channel today.
Reports are safe by design. They are public, but only fixed sections are included: device basics, git status, the queue, the config timeouts and the log tail. Token patterns and token lines are stripped, size is capped at 60 KB, and the token comes from your shell, not the block. The branch doesn't trigger CI.
Failures don't cost you a night. I can't promise zero failures. Every stop pushes its evidence and the next step is already decided.

The ladder

Run it tonight (phone plugged in, wakelock on), in Ubuntu. It's the same block every night, and I'll tell you if the IDS line ever changes. If the first lines say STOP, screenshot just that.
bash -c '
export GIT_PAGER=cat
IDS="LAUNCH-OLLAMA-DOWN LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER"
MODELS="opencode/deepseek-v4.1-flash-free opencode/glm-5.3-flash-free opencode/big-pickle"
WINDOW=32400; CAP=14400; IDLE=1800; TICK=30; MINLEFT=1800; GRACE=60
cd /sdcard/jarvis-repo || { echo "STOP: repo path missing"; exit 1; }
F=/root/.config/opencode/opencode.json
jq -e ".provider.opencode.options.chunkTimeout and .provider.opencode.options.timeout" "$F" > /dev/null || { echo "STOP: chunkTimeout or timeout missing in $F"; exit 1; }
OC=$(timeout 60 opencode debug config < /dev/null 2>/dev/null)
V=$(echo "$OC" | jq -r ".provider.opencode.options.chunkTimeout // \"missing\"" 2>/dev/null)
if [ -z "$V" ]; then echo "WARN: opencode debug config unavailable; config file checked only"; elif [ "$V" = missing ]; then echo "STOP: opencode does not resolve chunkTimeout"; exit 1; else echo "opencode resolved chunkTimeout=$V"; fi
timeout 20 opencode service set disabled true < /dev/null > /dev/null 2>&1 && echo "opencode: private server per command (persistent; undo with: opencode service set disabled false)" || echo "WARN: opencode service setting unsupported here"
START=$(date +%s); END=$((START+WINDOW)); IT=0; STALLS=0; BAD=""; WHY=""; DONE=""; L=/root/night_lines.txt; : > "$L"
redact() { sed -E "s/(gh[pousr]_[A-Za-z0-9]{20,}|github_pat_[A-Za-z0-9_]{20,}|sk-[A-Za-z0-9]{16,}|AKIA[A-Z0-9]{12,})/[REDACTED]/g; /GITHUB_TOKEN=|Authorization:|Bearer /d" | head -c 60000; }
push_report() { [ -n "$GITHUB_TOKEN" ] || { echo "WARN: no GITHUB_TOKEN, report kept at $1"; return 1; }; AP=https://api.github.com/repos/mhw77655-hue/Friday; HD="Authorization: Bearer $GITHUB_TOKEN"; if ! curl -s -f -m 30 -H "$HD" "$AP/git/ref/heads/reports/phone" > /dev/null 2>&1; then SH=$(curl -s -m 30 -H "$HD" "$AP/git/ref/heads/main" | jq -r .object.sha); curl -s -f -m 30 -X POST -H "$HD" "$AP/git/refs" -d "{\"ref\":\"refs/heads/reports/phone\",\"sha\":\"$SH\"}" > /dev/null 2>&1 || { echo "WARN: could not create the reports/phone branch, report kept at $1"; return 1; }; fi; printf "{\"message\":\"phone report %s\",\"branch\":\"reports/phone\",\"content\":\"%s\"}" "$2" "$(base64 -w0 "$1")" > /root/push_body.json; if curl -s -f -m 60 -X PUT -H "$HD" "$AP/contents/reports/$2" -d @/root/push_body.json > /dev/null 2>&1; then echo "pushed: https://github.com/mhw77655-hue/Friday/blob/reports/phone/reports/$2"; else echo "WARN: report push failed, kept at $1"; return 1; fi; }
status_report() { { echo "# phone report: $1 ($(date -u +%FT%TZ))"; echo "supervisor v3; ids: $IDS; models: $MODELS"; echo "## machine"; uname -m; nproc; grep -E "MemTotal|MemAvailable" /proc/meminfo; df -h /sdcard 2>/dev/null | tail -1; echo "## opencode"; opencode --version 2>&1 | head -1; jq -c ".provider.opencode.options" "$F"; echo "## repo"; git rev-parse --short HEAD; git status -sb | head -30; git log --oneline -8; git diff --stat | tail -15; echo "## queue"; bash .ralph/progress.sh | head -24; echo "## night so far"; cat "$L" 2>/dev/null; echo "## last ralph log (tail)"; tail -80 "$(ls -t .ralph/ralph_run_*.log 2>/dev/null | head -1)" 2>/dev/null; } 2>&1 | redact > "/root/report_$2.md"; push_report "/root/report_$2.md" "$2.md"; }
snap() { echo "$(git rev-parse HEAD) $(jq "[.userStories[]|select(.passes==true)]|length" .ralph/prd.json) $(git status --porcelain | md5sum | cut -c1-12)"; }
remaining() { R=0; for I in $IDS; do case " $DONE " in *" $I "*) ;; *) R=$((R+1));; esac; done; echo $R; }
check_done() { for I in $IDS; do case " $DONE " in *" $I "*) continue;; esac; jq -e ".userStories[] | select(.id==\"$I\") | .passes == true" .ralph/prd.json > /dev/null 2>&1 || continue; CMD=$(jq -r ".userStories[] | select(.id==\"$I\") | .verifyCommand" .ralph/prd.json); if timeout 2400 bash -c "$CMD" < /dev/null > /root/verify_$I.txt 2>&1; then DONE="$DONE $I"; echo "verified: $I" | tee -a "$L"; else echo "claimed closed but NOT verified: $I" | tee -a "$L"; fi; done; }
active() { [ $(( $(date +%s) - T0 )) -lt "$IDLE" ] && return 0; [ -n "$(find .ralph -maxdepth 1 -name "ralph_run_*.log" -newermt "@$T0" -newermt "$IDLE seconds ago" -print -quit 2>/dev/null)" ] && return 0; [ -n "$(find . -path ./.git -prune -o -path ./.ralph -prune -o -type f -newermt "@$T0" -newermt "$IDLE seconds ago" -print -quit 2>/dev/null)" ] && return 0; return 1; }
stop_run() { kill -TERM "$TP" 2>/dev/null; i=0; while kill -0 "$TP" 2>/dev/null && [ "$i" -lt "$GRACE" ]; do sleep 1; i=$((i+1)); done; kill -KILL -- "-$TP" 2>/dev/null; sleep 2; }
clear_lock() { [ -f .git/index.lock ] || return 0; [ "$(stat -c %Y .git/index.lock)" -ge "$T0" ] || { echo "WARN: index.lock predates this iteration; left in place" | tee -a "$L"; return 0; }; for k in 1 2 3; do if pgrep -x git > /dev/null; then sleep 5; else rm -f .git/index.lock; echo "removed an index.lock created during the killed run" | tee -a "$L"; return 0; fi; done; echo "WARN: git is running; index.lock left in place" | tee -a "$L"; }
pick() { M=""; for X in $MODELS; do case " $BAD " in *" $X "*) continue;; esac; if bash .ralph/ralph.sh --preflight-only --model "$X" > /root/pf.txt 2>&1; then M="$X"; echo "USING $M"; return 0; fi; echo "preflight failed: $X"; BAD="$BAD $X"; done; return 1; }
demote() { R=""; for x in $MODELS; do [ "$x" = "$M" ] || R="$R $x"; done; MODELS="${R# } $M"; }
git status -sb | head -8
if [ -f .git/index.lock ]; then AG=$(( $(date +%s) - $(stat -c %Y .git/index.lock) )); if [ "$AG" -gt 600 ] && ! pgrep -x git > /dev/null; then rm -f .git/index.lock; echo "removed an old index.lock (${AG}s old, no git running)"; else echo "STOP: index.lock is recent or git is running"; exit 1; fi; fi
status_report "start" "start_$(date +%m%d_%H%M)"
T0=$(date +%s); check_done
if [ "$(remaining)" -eq 0 ]; then WHY="all queued stories already verified"
elif pick; then
while [ "$(remaining)" -gt 0 ]; do
  LEFT=$((END-$(date +%s)))
  if [ "$LEFT" -lt "$MINLEFT" ]; then WHY="window ended with stories still open"; break; fi
  IT=$((IT+1)); CP=$LEFT; [ "$CP" -gt "$CAP" ] && CP=$CAP
  NV0=$(echo $DONE | wc -w); B=$(snap); T0=$(date +%s); STALLED=0
  timeout -k "$GRACE" "$CP" bash .ralph/ralph.sh --tool opencode --model "$M" 1 < /dev/null &
  TP=$!
  while kill -0 "$TP" 2>/dev/null; do sleep "$TICK"; if ! active; then STALLED=1; stop_run; break; fi; done
  wait "$TP"; C=$?
  KILLED=0; if [ "$STALLED" = 1 ] || [ "$C" = 124 ] || [ "$C" = 137 ]; then KILLED=1; kill -KILL -- "-$TP" 2>/dev/null; sleep 1; SV=$(pgrep -g "$TP" 2>/dev/null | wc -l); [ "$SV" -gt 0 ] && echo "WARN: $SV processes of the stopped run are still alive" | tee -a "$L"; clear_lock; fi
  A=$(snap); check_done; NV=$(echo $DONE | wc -w)
  status_report "after iteration $IT" "iter_${IT}_$(date +%m%d_%H%M)"
  if [ "$KILLED" = 1 ]; then
    STALLS=$((STALLS+1)); echo "iteration $IT: stalled or hit the cap on $M ($STALLS in a row)" | tee -a "$L"
    [ "$(remaining)" -eq 0 ] && break
    if [ "$STALLS" -ge 2 ]; then WHY="two stalls in a row"; break; fi
    demote; pick || { WHY="no model left after a stall"; break; }
    continue
  fi
  STALLS=0
  if [ "$NV" -gt "$NV0" ]; then echo "iteration $IT VERIFIED $((NV-NV0)) more story on $M" | tee -a "$L"
  elif [ "$A" != "$B" ]; then echo "iteration $IT partial progress on $M (changes, nothing newly verified)" | tee -a "$L"
  else WHY="iteration $IT changed nothing"; echo "iteration $IT: changed nothing on $M" | tee -a "$L"; break; fi
done
else WHY="no model passed preflight"; fi
[ -z "$WHY" ] && WHY="all queued stories verified"
OUT=/sdcard/Download/ralph_night_$(date +%m%d_%H%M).txt
{ echo "RESULT: $WHY"; echo "supervisor v3, started $(date -d @$START +%H:%M), finished $(date +%H:%M), iterations $IT"; echo "verified by their own verify commands:$DONE"; cat "$L"; for I in $IDS; do case " $DONE " in *" $I "*) ;; *) echo "--- $I NOT verified; last verify output:"; tail -8 /root/verify_$I.txt 2>/dev/null;; esac; done; echo "--- queue"; bash .ralph/progress.sh | head -24; echo "--- git"; git status -sb | head -12; git log --oneline -6; git diff --stat | tail -10; echo "--- last ralph log lines"; tail -40 "$(ls -t .ralph/ralph_run_*.log 2>/dev/null | head -1)"; } > "$OUT" 2>&1
redact < "$OUT" > /root/final_report.md; push_report /root/final_report.md "night_$(date +%m%d_%H%M).md"
echo "RESULT: $WHY -> $OUT (it is also on GitHub, nothing to send)"
'

What I think you've finally got right
The most important change is that you're removing yourself from the repetitive development process.
Your role becomes:

Start the development cycle.
Authorize installation when Android requires it.

The system handles the repetitive investigation, coding, building, verification and reporting.
There are several particularly good decisions here:
The launch-first priority. There's no point building voice, seed capabilities or a more sophisticated Foundry if Friday cannot reliably start.
The report branch. A persistent, automatically updated reporting channel is a meaningful improvement. You should not have to manually collect logs every time something breaks.
The bounded overnight run. Time limits, model preflight, inactivity detection, verification and evidence collection are much better than letting an agent run indefinitely.
The staged rename. Keeping it on a separate branch with an acceptance gate protects the existing application. That's the right approach for a change affecting hundreds of files.

Four things I would fix before running the block
1. The public report is not sufficiently safe yet
This is my biggest objection.
Look at this line:
tail -80 "$(ls -t .ralph/ralph_run_*.log 2>/dev/null | head -1)"

You're uploading the last 80 lines of an arbitrary agent log.
Those lines could contain prompts, user data, credentials, API responses or information that the regular-expression redactor doesn't recognize.
The same problem exists here:
jq -c ".provider.opencode.options" "$F"

You're exporting an entire configuration object when you only need a few timeout values.
The current redactor catches several common token formats. It cannot guarantee that everything sensitive is removed.
My requirement: publish structured diagnostic information, not raw logs. Start with an explicit allowlist. Report error categories, exit codes, timestamps, model names and selected sanitized diagnostic messages. Keep raw logs private unless you deliberately inspect and approve them.
There's another security detail: this line:
HD="Authorization: Bearer $GITHUB_TOKEN"

passes the token to curl as a command-line argument. I would avoid exposing credentials through process arguments. Use an authenticated GitHub CLI workflow or pass the header through a protected input mechanism.
2. The script does not actually restrict Ralph to your two stories
This is a functional issue.
You define:
IDS="LAUNCH-OLLAMA-DOWN LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER"

But the builder is started with:
bash .ralph/ralph.sh --tool opencode --model "$M" 1

The IDS variable only determines which stories the supervisor checks for completion. It is not passed to Ralph as an instruction to work exclusively on those stories.
Unless the existing Ralph implementation and queue guarantee those are the only eligible stories, it could work on another story.
I would add a preflight gate that checks the actual eligible queue before starting. The run should stop unless the intended scope is confirmed.
The supervisor must control the work, not merely monitor whether two particular stories eventually get completed.
3. The reporting channel must be a prerequisite, not a warning
Currently:
[ -n "$GITHUB_TOKEN" ] || {
  echo "WARN: no GITHUB_TOKEN, report kept at $1"
  return 1
}

The script continues even when reporting fails.
That contradicts the most important promise of this plan: you no longer need to carry reports manually.
I would require:

Token availability verified before starting.
Permission to create and update the reporting branch verified.
The initial report successfully uploaded.
The report URL printed and confirmed accessible.

If this fails, stop before starting Ralph.
Also, verify the actual GitHub Actions workflow triggers. The script doesn't establish that pushing to reports/phone cannot trigger CI. That depends on the workflow configuration.
4. Don't permanently change OpenCode configuration without restoring it
This line deserves attention:
opencode service set disabled true

The script explicitly acknowledges that this setting persists, but it never restores the previous state automatically.
I would either remove this operation or record the original setting and restore it through an exit trap, including when the script is interrupted.
You don't want an overnight supervisor to leave your development environment in a different state from the one it found.

One more distinction about the reporting
The initial report can demonstrate that information travels from your Ubuntu/phone environment to GitHub.
But the current report is not yet a full phone-diagnostics system.
It collects some machine information, repository information, queue state, OpenCode configuration and Ralph logs. It does not yet collect Friday's actual crash trace or comprehensively establish Android version, installed APK version and Ollama availability.
That's fine for the first reporting-channel test. Just don't mistake that first test for proof that the future phone diagnostics are complete.

2. The Cloud Builder is not yet proven
This is where I would be careful.
Claude reports that the runner and installer passed simulated scenarios, including stalled models, recovery, relay, missing permissions and damaged input. That's useful engineering work. But the same report explicitly says the real runner environment and model tool usage were not tested. 
There are three implementation problems I spotted in the proposed runner.
First: credential isolation.
The workflow gives the job contents: write, actions: write and issues: write. It also exposes provider API keys to the same job running OpenCode. The description says it uses only the temporary GitHub token, but the final workflow actually includes NVIDIA and OpenRouter secrets. 
Because the coding agent can invoke tools and shell commands, I would not expose powerful repository credentials to its execution environment. The trusted orchestration process should retain those permissions; the agent should receive only what it needs.
Second: the CI pump and final delivery have a coordination bug.
In the supplied runner, finish() terminates the background CI pump before pushing the final commit to main. Then deliver() looks for a successful build of that final commit.
The conversation explicitly states that pushes made with the GitHub job token don't automatically trigger CI. Consequently, this sequence can leave the final commit without a build and prevent APK delivery.
The pump also counts any workflow run associated with a commit, rather than specifically checking whether build.yml has run.
Third: protecting the previous APK.
The delivery function deletes the existing friday-latest release before creating the replacement. If the replacement fails, the last working release could be lost.
These are concrete problems in the supplied code, not reasons to abandon the cloud approach.
